<meta>This page continues the prologue of Euripides' tragedy "Hecuba." While the previous page established Polydorus' identity and his father's attempt to save him, this page details his brutal murder by his host, Polymestor, and his current state as a wandering ghost. The left column contains a complex Byzantine grammatical commentary on the nature of numbers and order, likely used by students studying the text.</meta>

### Grammatical Commentary on Quantity and Order

The triad <note>The number three</note> is derived from what, and how is it named? One must interpret the nature of the third and the dual in terms of the power of **order**. It is said that the unit <note>the "monad"</note> is consistent with itself, just as we say "the one" and "the same." For it is clear that everything has a **first**, a **second**, and a **third**.

The feminine forms of these words are derived from the female gender: "the first," "the second," and "the third." <note>The Greek text here explains how numerical adjectives change their endings to match feminine nouns.</note> The term "third" is a derivative word. It signifies something like "the third one" or similar. It is said that "the third" and the rest are derived from the feminine forms.

The "third" is taken from the middle. We say "the third" when there is a "fourth" following in this sequence. If we speak of parts, we ask: "what is it?" in terms of position. For example, a part of a part.

A definition in common speech concerns "how much" <term>Quantity: in Greek 'poson', referring to things that can be counted</term>, "how big" <term>Magnitude: in Greek 'pelikon', referring to the size of continuous things like lines or space</term>, and the relationship between them. The "third" relates to the "how many" of an **order**, just as "how much" relates to "how many days old" or "four days old." 

We distinguish between things that are **continuous** <note>like a liquid or a line</note> and things that are **discrete** <note>like a pile of stones or a group of people</note>. All things are compared against magnitude—that which is continuous.

<column-break/>

<meta>This page continues the opening scene of Euripides' tragedy, "Hecuba." On the preceding page, the ghost of Polydorus concluded his speech, warning of the impending tragedy. Now, the focus shifts to his mother, Hecuba, the former Queen of Troy. Once a powerful sovereign, she is now a prisoner of war. These lines (59–81) capture her "monody"—a solo emotional song—as she is led out of the Greek tents. The heavy presence of "scholia" (ancient and medieval commentaries) in the margins and between the lines indicates that this was a study text, helping a student navigate the complex poetic language and mythological references.</meta>

<margin>
He calls the Earth the mother of "dark-winged dreams" because the shadow of the earth does not appear below ground, through which they see the visions of dreams; or according to another interpretation, because from the hidden depths of the earth come the appearances of these visions. Sleep comes from the earth, and from sleep come dreams; therefore, dreams are born from the earth.
</margin>

The manuscript includes extensive Byzantine commentary explaining the metaphorical language of Hecuba’s frailty and the mythological origins of dreams.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the opening scene of Euripides' tragedy, *Hecuba*. We are currently in the "monody" (solo song) of the protagonist, Hecuba, the fallen Queen of Troy. She is reeling from a prophetic nightmare about her children, Polydorus and Polyxena. The page layout reflects a typical Byzantine study manuscript: the central poetic text is surrounded by interlinear "glosses" (short synonyms or grammatical notes) and "scholia" (longer explanatory comments) in the right-hand column. The red cross and invocation at the top indicate the scribe's prayer for a successful transcription.</meta>

<term>stichomythia</term> <note>A technique in ancient Greek drama where characters exchange single lines of dialogue in rapid succession, usually during moments of high tension or debate.</note>

Note how he speaks as if robbers weaving flax also weave crafts for the purpose of passing through the span of life; for it is a great thing to strongly educate the safety of one’s age, so that nothing of the fortunate man is hated before God; which never results in hatred or shame for those to whom he leads a better line toward the greater; so that he might prepare those who have cast off the things of the soul; that the beautiful things may again be shared brilliantly, knowing himself as if our hope of power comes from the father, so that the destructive evil might be made an associate of death.

The text here reflects a Byzantine-era transcription, likely a student’s copy, featuring interlinear explanations (glosses) and extensive marginal commentary on the vocabulary of the tragedy.</meta>

<summary>Hecuba delivers a sharp rebuttal to Polymestor's testimony, arguing that his claim of killing her son Polydorus to assist the Greek war effort is a fabrication. --- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Hecuba’s rebuttal against Polymestor. In the previous section, she dismantled his claim that he killed her son Polydorus out of loyalty to the Greeks. Here, she exposes his true motive: greed. She argues that if he were truly a friend to the Greeks, he would have brought the gold to them during their ten-year struggle at Troy, rather than hoarding it and murdering his guest once the city fell.</meta>

### -> Argument of the Drama Electra <-
<note>Although the scribe titled this section "Electra," the text that follows is actually the "Hypothesis" (plot summary) for Euripides' play "Orestes."</note>

<margin>Argument of Electra</margin> **O**restes, seeking vengeance for the murder of his father <gloss>father</gloss>, killed his mother in secret. Although he had acted boldly without being burdened <note>meaning he did not hesitate during the act</note>, he was hounded by the Furies after the sentence of justice. While the divine spirit of his father <gloss>father</gloss> sought justice and accused [him], meanwhile by the common opinion of the city... <note>text missing or fragmented here</note>... and by the common opinion of the city... because of the pursuit of justice... Menelaus, returning from Troy, brought Helen in by night, but arrived himself by day. When Orestes begged him for help, Tyndareus spoke against it; Menelaus was wary because of him, fearing the Greeks would think he aided such men. The assembly decided to put Orestes to death, and Menelaus failed to aid him. Thus, with Pylades and Electra taking counsel, they decided to kill Helen and seize Hermione as leverage against the Greeks on account of Helen. They attempted this, but Helen vanished. Pylades and Orestes then threatened Hermione. Menelaus arrived and witnessed them, but did not know the murder was done. Then Apollo appeared from the heavens and said that Helen had been snatched up to the heights by him. He ordered Orestes to marry Hermione and Pylades to marry Electra. Having been purified of the murder, Orestes [unclear] to rule Argos. The Chorus is made up of Argive women who offer comfort to Orestes. The scene of the play is set in Argos; the drama has a more "comic" <note>original: "κωμικωτέραν" (kōmikōteran) — in ancient criticism, this meant a play with a happy resolution or a "double ending" where the good are rewarded and the bad punished, rather than a tragedy ending in death.</note> conclusion.

<summary>This page concludes the final lines of Euripides' Hecuba and provides a "Hypothesis" or plot summary for the play Orestes, though it is curiously mislabeled as Electra by the scribe.</summary>
<keywords>Euripides, Hecuba, Orestes, Electra, Hypothesis, Menelaus, Pylades, Apollo, Furies, Greek Tragedy, Argos, Helen of Troy</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the transition between the conclusion of Euripides' tragedy 'Hecuba' and the introductory summary for his play 'Orestes'. The previous page concluded the dialogue between Hecuba and Polymestor regarding the dark prophecies of their future.</meta>

The scene of the drama is the royal palace in Argos. Orestes <gloss>of him</gloss> lies there, afflicted by madness and resting upon a small bed. Electra sits beside him at his feet. There is great uncertainty about what the city council will propose. She sits there to care for her brother as if she were a constant star <note>original: "ἄστρον" (astron) — the scribe uses this metaphor to describe Electra's unwavering watch over her brother</note> sitting by him. The Argives are deliberating because of the murder of the mother <note>Clytemnestra, killed by her children Orestes and Electra</note>, debating whether to exile them from the land or rather to sentence them to death. Knowing these things, they would not have provided a chorus of women <note>The scribe suggests that the siblings' isolation was so great that a chorus shouldn't have been possible</note>. In the midst of this, Menelaus arrived from Troy by night, fearing that he might be noticed by the famous citizens. He sent Helen ahead to the palace before dawn because <gloss>something</gloss> he feared they might hear of the current situation on the stage. He will come to Menelaus; except for Pylades, standing before a wretched place somewhere. The god <note>Apollo</note>, through the events following the slaughter of both, under the <unclear>previous</unclear> <unclear>for</unclear> custom of the one, casting away hopes and being condemned to death. Regarding the murder of Helen as a fitting arrangement <note>original: "ἁρμονίην" (harmonien) — here suggesting a cosmic balance or "harmony" through her death</note>, for which we sacrifice; Menelaus, though present, did not help. This was prevented, however, through her abduction. The tragedy concludes in this way: she <note>Electra</note> would lament by the small bed. Who among men is to be changed for us? For fear at that time is the "machine" <note>original: "μηχανή" (mechane) — the plot device or engine</note> of the drama, and it ends in joy <gloss>m</gloss>, reconciling Electra with Menelaus. He [the god] also sets things right for Helen, revealing her in heaven. For it was not fitting for her to be carried away as she ascended. While one path leads to murders and the other to natural growth and such things for us, these outcomes are revealed from the many causes of their composition. All the writing is for the sake of distinction. The rest and the following parts of the drama are as follows:—

Suffering <term>pathos: a state of being acted upon or a painful experience</term>: that which happens, whether a blow of a sword or something else of that kind. Misfortune <term>symphora: literally "a bringing together" of events, often used for calamity</term> is not said of the one who acts, but rather of the one who suffers. Just as we speak of "grieving" from the perspective of the one who is grieved, or "giving" from the perspective of the one who gives a gift. From this comes "fortune" <note>original: "τύχη" (tyche)</note>. Suffering is called "pathos" from the perspective of the victim... or something of that sort. Even if a person is grieved within themselves, it is called this... one expects it as something better, which is "fortune"... or from the "tally" <note>original: "συμβόλου" (symbolou)</note> of these things... or the giving of substance and such things. It does not happen otherwise... it happens to their children through certain causes which are hidden in this <unclear>race-course</unclear>. With the gathering and the disease, they are brought down. Know well that "suffering" is most often called a blow or an evil.
"But the blessed one": returning to the one on whom he inflicts punishment. Since if he inflicts punishment on him alone... if indeed those things are not good, but for many... for "the blessed one" means the blessed one is worthy. He fathered him, and indeed many actors do not fulfill the role. "The blessed one" is used here by the one who is "not blessed." Here it is used by way of subtraction <note>The commentator is explaining the irony of calling Tantalus "blessed" given his eternal punishment</note>. Tantalus does not move through the rocks themselves, but because of them he hardly holds on; through these things, he is in a worse state. Since "blessed" is a word used in justice, they are divided from... and that in the others...
...

throughout every injustice; the part is indifferent, for it is God who distinguishes. And we shall communicate with him as Homer does; for we shall communicate with the one who has perished from the things they received. — For why, in the face of nature, did he nurture from one act of impiety? <meta>This page continues the "paraphrasis" (rephrasing or commentary) and the primary text of Euripides' tragedy "Orestes." Having established the tension of Electra watching over her sick brother, the text now moves into the "Parodos" (the entrance of the Chorus). <meta>The previous page concluded Orestes's defense where he argued that his mother’s crime of husband-killing would set a dangerous precedent for all Greek men if left unpunished. This page continues his defense, moving from legalistic arguments to a theological shift: blaming the god Apollo for commanding the murder. The scribe includes extensive scholarly commentary (scholia) in the first column and the poetic text of Euripides's *Orestes* (lines 591–606) with student glosses in the second.</meta>

That it is also this way among the poets is clear from the **Samians** of Hesiod <note>original: σαμίων. This likely refers to a lost work attributed to Hesiod or perhaps a reference to the "Samians" of the playwright Menander mentioned below.</note>. In the Dionysiac festivals, persuasion is a matter of judgment, or as in Menander:
These things show that life is a **blessed age**.
Through this "ancient life" they are enslaved, so that these are blessed, but <unclear>passing ages</unclear> which he honors and does not <unclear>settle</unclear>. Thus also is the **blessed life**, of which not even the ancient things are remembered. For the term "ancient" does not imply a certain forgetfulness, but rather that one does not even settle down because of the **blessed living** derived from it. Some say "from of old" or in those things where the "all" enjoys itself.
And regarding the "ancient" or that which he enjoys, he does not have it, nor is he limited by it. So that since such a thing is more blessed, he does not even attain it. But perhaps through the act of enjoying it, it falls together as a beneficial hearing.
I agree that he is fortunate. Regarding the phrase "possessing the gods," it refers to those who are honored by the gods.
To fall together with those who have not suffered piously and with the pious; and for him who cares for these things to also have life through action. And just as nothing is known to itself according to things not revered, it is beneficial even with the pains of the terrible things against the gods.
From these things themselves, truth is no longer natural. It seems that the "all," not having attained an age of ages, was conceived as "at the foot," and the immortals show it to no one. Therefore, he gives this out as if from ordinary men. For this reason, he [did not?] when they were not even at his viewing.
And just as the work of the poets is a work from that which is with this "all," I say they did not have it.

# The Caution of Menelaus

<margin>

<term>Orestes</term>: The son of Agamemnon, on trial for killing his mother Clytemnestra.
<term>Pylades</term>: Orestes' loyal friend who accompanies him even to his trial.
<term>Herald</term>: An official who opens the assembly proceedings.
<term>Danaans</term>: An ancient name for the Greeks.
<term>Crowd</term>: The citizens of Argos gathered to vote on the death penalty.
<term>Messenger</term>: An anonymous character who narrates the trial to Electra.
<term>Riddles</term>: Used here to mean clear or hidden meanings in the political debate.
<term>Mother-killer</term>: The specific charge of matricide leveled against Orestes.

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the choral lament following the death sentence passed upon Orestes and Electra. While the previous page focused on the trial's injustice and the loyalty of friends like Pylades, this section—marked as the "Antistrophe"—shifts to a more cosmic and ancestral perspective. It invokes the gods and the tragic history of the House of Atreus, specifically the curse that has plagued the family since the time of Pelops. The marginalia and interlinear notes (scholia) on this page are particularly dense, reflecting a medieval scholar's attempt to parse the difficult poetic language and the psychological state of the "Spartan-madness" or fury driving the characters.</meta>

# Antistrophe
<note>In Greek tragedy, the Antistrophe is the second part of a choral song, following the Strophe and mirroring its complex rhythmic structure.</note>

<margin>
I accept that I must
act; and that I
must remain hidden:

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the choral song from the tragedy Orestes by Euripides. Following the previous page’s focus on the immediate terror of the death sentence, the Chorus now expands its lament to the cosmic scale. They reflect on the instability of human life and trace the current misery of Orestes and Electra back to their ancestors—specifically the crimes of Tantalus and Pelops. The marginalia here attempt to explain Euripides’ famous and controversial physical theory that the Sun is actually a molten rock suspended in the heavens by golden chains.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the tense scene where Orestes, Electra, and Pylades perform a ritual invocation of their dead father, Agamemnon. Having finalized their plan to kill Helen, they now stand at his metaphorical "tomb" (or rather, address his spirit within the palace) to demand his help. This "triple prayer" mirrors the famous scene in Aeschylus's *Libation Bearers*, but here it is used to justify a new act of vengeance.</meta>

# Commentary on the Monody

It is not, then, of the present age <note>The commentator is likely discussing the archaic or foreign style of the slave's speech.</note> to speak rightly toward Helen; enough has been said regarding the fulfillment. For before the piteous labor, as if looking up at both. From where, either helping or fully dividing, he purified what were very deceptive phantoms. For he did not see the reed <note>original: "σκάλαμον" - likely a reference to a writing reed or a structural element of a loom</note> as some ancient phantastic thing. For it was not because of fear that he was obeyed through indolence, but through the light. He happened to be settled with the Phrygians <note>The Trojans</note> just as according to barbarian customs. Moving again the robes concerning the singers. Again from the locks of hair, the skins, this of the worms... <unclear>...</unclear>

Behold, she herself was there at the histories of the purple cloths, holding them. As if upon the threads of his fingers, the spindle of the linen, she hastened to the... O, may you be blessed! The shuttles <note>original: "σκαφαὶ" - literally "skiffs," used here as a metaphor for the weaving shuttles</note> are like offerings upon the altars, not of incense, but through the spoils of the Phrygians. I say that with their minds they prepare the purple gifts for Clytemnestra <note>Helen was weaving a gift for her sister, the deceased Queen Clytemnestra, when Orestes attacked.</note> +

The text is a mix of the original poetic lines and "scholia"—medieval commentary written between the lines or in the margins to help readers understand the difficult ancient vocabulary. There are also later Byzantine additions, such as references to St. George, showing how later Christian scribes interacted with this pagan play.</meta>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>Continuity Note: This page marks the beginning of Euripides' play *The Phoenician Women* (Phoenissae). It follows a missing or illegible section (Page 69) that originally separated the end of *Orestes* from this new work. In the Byzantine manuscript tradition, plays were often preceded by a "Hypothesis"—a scholarly introduction and plot summary likely written by ancient critics like Aristophanes of Byzantium.</meta>

### The Plot of the Phoenician Women

Eteocles, having taken over the kingship of the Thebans, deprives his brother Polynices of his rightful share of the rule. <note>original: "τοῦ μέρους" — literally "the part" or "the portion," referring to their agreement to rule in alternate years.</note> Consequently, Polynices goes as an exile to Argos. There, after some days, he married the daughter of King Adrastus. Being eager to restore Polynices to his homeland, Adrastus raises a massive army from among the Argives and, along with seven captains, stands against the city of Thebes. 

Their mother, Jocasta, being eager for a truce, persuades Polynices to enter the city by means of her influence over her children. Having trusted the promises of his mother, he appeared within the city walls. However, because Eteocles would not accept the terms, a slaughter of the sons takes place. <note>In the play, the brothers kill each other in single combat.</note> After the slaughter, Jocasta herself kills herself. The leaders of the Argives fell in battle; after the fighting had lasted many days, the brothers killed one another. After these events, Creon took over the rule. He buries Eteocles, but he cast out the body of Polynices to remain unburied. Antigone, having secretly buried her brother, was discovered and punished by Creon.

---

**The chorus consists of Phoenician women. The prologue is delivered by Jocasta.**
**The characters of the drama:**

| | | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Jocasta** | **Tutor** <note>original: "Παιδαγωγός" — a slave tasked with the education and protection of children.</note> | **Antigone** | **Chorus** |
| **Polynices** | **Eteocles** | **Creon** | **Tiresias** |
| **Menoeceus** | **Messenger** | **Second Messenger** <note>original: "Ἐξάγγελος" — specifically a messenger who reports what happened inside the palace.</note> | **Oedipus** |

<note>original Greek vocabulary provided in transcription: Eteocles, Polynices, Jocasta, Phoenician Women, Euripides, Creon, Antigone, Hypothesis (Plot Summary)</note>

| Technical Term | English Translation | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Φοινίκια | Phoenicia | The coastal homeland of the Chorus (modern Lebanon/Syria). |
| Ἄρης | Ares | The god of war and personification of bloody conflict. |
| Ἰνάχου | Of Inachus | A river god and father of Io; represents the Chorus's ancient Greek roots. |
| θεόθεν | From the gods | Suggests that the current events are divinely ordained. |
| αἰῶνα | Age / Eternity | Refers to a lifespan, an era, or the eternal nature of the gods. |
| Φοίβου | Of Phoebus | A title for Apollo, highlighting his role as a god of light and truth. |
| δούριος | Spear-bearing | A poetic description of a warrior or a wooden object (like a spear or the Trojan horse). |
| σκοποὶ | Watchers | Guardians or observers, often used for gods looking down on mortals. |

<summary>The Chorus reacts with terror to the arrival of Ares (war) at the gates of Thebes, lamenting the shared suffering caused by the conflict between the two brothers. They invoke their ancient lineage from Io to explain their deep connection to the city's fate.</summary>
<keywords>Ares, Thebes, Phoenicia, Io, Inachus, Phoebus Apollo, civil war, fate, tragedy</keywords>

<meta>This page indicates a shift in the manuscript’s focus. While the previous page provided a commentary on Euripides' *Hippolytus*, Page 78 transitions into a series of "gnomai"—moral maxims or proverbs—extracted from his other tragedies, specifically *Hecuba* and *The Phoenician Women*. The dialogue is structured as a "stichomythia" (a line-for-line exchange) between characters identified as Polydorus (the ghost of Hecuba's son) and Hecuba herself. The text appears to be a Byzantine educational tool, where classical tragic lines are repurposed to teach ethics and rhetoric, complete with interlinear explanations of difficult terms.</meta>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous classical commentary into the "Christus Patiens" (The Suffering Christ), a 12th-century Byzantine drama. This play is a "cento," a literary work composed of verses stripped from ancient Greek tragedies—primarily those of Euripides—and reassembled to tell the story of the Crucifixion and Resurrection. The dialogue here features a conversation between a Messenger (often labeled as the Poet or "Apokrithes") and Joseph of Arimathea, discussing the nature of suffering and divine wisdom.</meta>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Christus Patiens" (The Suffering Christ), a Byzantine drama from the 12th century. The play is a "cento," meaning it is composed of lines taken from ancient Greek plays—specifically Euripides—to tell the story of the Passion. While the original Greek tragedy featured the warring brothers Eteocles and Polynices, here the lines are reassigned to characters like the Messenger, Joseph of Arimathea, or the Virgin Mary to discuss the unfolding tragedy of the Crucifixion.</meta>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of Euripides' "Erechtheus" to another of his tragedies, "The Phoenician Women" (Phoenissae). It specifically focuses on the dialogue between Creon and his son Menoeceus. In this scene, the prophet Tiresias has revealed that Menoeceus must be sacrificed to save the city of Thebes. Creon, desperate to save his son, is urging him to flee to a distant land, while Menoeceus feigns agreement before eventually deciding to sacrifice himself for the state.</meta>

<summary>This page contains a Byzantine-era paraphrase of Menoeceus' final speech from Euripides' "Phoenician Women," where the hero resolves to sacrifice himself at the altar to save Thebes. <meta>This page continues the transcription and commentary of a poetic text, likely related to the "Alexandra" of Lycophron. While the previous page focused on the self-sacrifice of Menoeceus in Euripides' "Phoenician Women," this page shifts toward a more obscure, riddle-like style of tragedy or epic, typical of the Hellenistic period. It discusses the "army of Pandion" (Athens) and various mythical or geographic metaphors.</meta>

<margin>
As for "if he should pass through the dance," that is, if Pandion's <note>Pandion was a legendary King of Athens; his "army" or "people" refers to the Athenians.</note> army should take you up, and the cities will set up a dance and rejoice forever at your destruction. Regarding the phrase "of the Athenians," instead of "of Pandion," it clearly refers to the people of the Romans. <note>The commentator here makes a late historical connection, identifying the "Athenians" of the text with the Roman people of his own time.</note> And what is this: "having wrestled through such great toil for this much time"? The multitude of the Athenians, that of Pandion. And "the cities [produced] a much-toiled offspring." And "still you sing as if you do not keep the word silent." "Callyopes" <note>original: καλλιόπων (kalliopōn)</note> refers to the beautiful voices, as Lycophron <note>Lycophron was a 3rd-century BCE poet known for the "Alexandra," an extremely difficult and obscure poem written in riddles.</note> calls them. It wanders with a thousand wings, and with the "mostoi" <note>original: μόστοις (mostois) — an obscure term interpreted by the commentator below as "murders" or "slaughters."</note> which again, like women, will inhabit life, just as the Persians did after the wars. Which cities—instead of the inhabitants of the cities—with you, the day [becomes] again a greater maiden; that child shall not be left behind. Regarding the "mostoi," it means the murders. "New [of] places" refers to the existing places, or those granted by God. As for "the flowering rush" <note>original: βούτομον (boutomon)</note>, he is speaking of the Isthmus. <note>The Isthmus of Corinth.</note>
</margin>

<summary>This page contains a complex mix of Euripides' dialogue and extensive medieval scholarly commentary (scholia) concerning Menoeceus' decision to sacrifice himself for Thebes. It explores the themes of civic duty, the "race of the dragon" (the Spartoi), and the tension between Creon's fatherly love and the city's need for a savior.</summary>

+
Not at all fitting; not well-sounding; not pleasing. From the word "fitting" <note>original: "ἄρτια" (artia)</note>; the word which the man before God appears to be. Not because of the grief of the mind; nor does he speak according to the melody. Because I shall not say what I announce according to your wishes, but with the weight of the whole matter. Because they are not fitting, and I care for these things. For that which concerns your fortune, which he has not told me or those hearing you; for the speech is fitting. So he will say things that are not pleasant for men to hear; because the things of the gods are not pleasant to hear; bringing the speech to where it still remains; it signifies the things ordered next; that after the greater good, it comes through the form of the tenth fortune <note>A Byzantine scholarly classification of tragic outcomes</note>. For to have hopes is the hope of men; I do not have the same hope as the one concerning the actual event. From the form of your fortune; for what is prayed for is not enough for me. His own things are no longer enough; and when it comes to the things of fortune. Thus the things of the divinity; and the things of the divinity are for the same divinity, either the whole or the terrible; thus we would wish not a human fate again the same things; because suddenly a divinity—a god—would have to use greater words. <meta>This page transitions from the previous account of "The Phoenician Women" to a Byzantine prose paraphrase (metaphrasis) of Euripides’ tragedy "Hecuba." The focus shifts to the horrific climax where Polymestor, the King of Thrace, recounts how the Trojan women lured him and his sons into their tents, murdered his children, and blinded him. The style is characteristic of the 14th-century scholar Manuel Moschopulos, who simplified classical Greek for medieval students. The interlinear glosses provide synonyms to make the poetic language of the original play more accessible.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the adaptation of Euripidean tragedy into a Byzantine Christian context, specifically following the structure of the *Christus Patiens* (The Suffering Christ). The dialogue, though labeled with the names of Hecuba and Polyxena from the play *Hecuba*, is intended to be read by a medieval audience as a dialogue between the Virgin Mary and those surrounding the Crucifixion. The themes of burial, maternal grief, and "noble" sacrifice are central here.</meta>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page represents a sharp departure from the previous page's transcription of Sophocles' *Oedipus Tyrannus*. While the preceding text focused on the tragic exile of a legendary king, this page contains an imperial administrative or legal document from the Byzantine era, specifically referencing the reign of Emperor Manuel I Komnenos. Such transitions are common in "miscellany" manuscripts where various texts were bound together for study.</meta>

# + By the right hand of the all-powerful among kings, Manuel the Great, by the grace of Christ our God, Emperor and Commander of the Army +

† It is the way of wise and compassionate kings to speak these pleasant words, things which shine upon you like the sun. He who reveals all things to everyone, whether regarding the present matter or the concerns of others, I believe it is beneficial for those <margin><image-desc>A small, stylized sketch of a bird or winged creature is drawn in the right margin next to the first paragraph.</image-desc></margin> under your rule. Indeed, to swear by your power—for everyone who has sworn to it in the past—is even now a matter of shared purpose. If one does not strive to become god-like, they should at least be equal in the common pursuit of stability. It is necessary to say that building on nothing is useless; therefore, the stability of the inhabited world <note>original: "oikoumene"</note> required what was beneficial. The oath is innate and desires to be directed toward those who swear it, yet nothing is discarded. Here, rising again, I must tell you: why is this being read again? As for the companion of the oath, he is likely not blameless in our eyes, looking toward a single portion. But the son-in-law <note>This likely refers to a political relative, perhaps Béla III of Hungary, who was Manuel I's intended heir for a time.</note> living in the same house and seeing the reasons, enjoys the benefits. For what if he holds the commands of marriage but does not see the other? He performs these things through those who see. He who is fittingly, but not truly, in need would be for those who endure. For I say, of necessity, for a child you have the testament <note>original: "diatheken"</note> through divine matters, wisely administered. As everything in his own kingdom is managed by a king from time to time, the successor to the scepters <note>A poetic term for the imperial throne.</note> ought not to give any oath. For if concerning these matters, he should be free from suspicion; and one should advise others in this way, unless he should put on a mask. But that blessed one, the thrice-blessed <note>A standard formula for a deceased emperor.</note> dwelling upon the proper word, says that this too is useful <gloss>not</gloss>; and the neighbor again? He calls him by building otherwise. For at the same time he agrees, as he said, regarding the alliances. Indeed, every other fellowship exists in a certain way <gloss>as</gloss> he says; for he says that even these are not to be exempted. For the oath is said to be nothing, especially for those whose heart is hardened. But where this does not prevail, he is supported in this with goodness. Where after death he also acquired it for his brothers, the most holy president <note>In this context, "proedros" refers to the Archbishop or high ecclesiastical official of the city.</note> of Dyrrhachium <note>Dyrrhachium (modern Durrës, Albania) was a vital Byzantine Adriatic port and military stronghold.</note> certainly expects those who follow him to receive a penalty, so as not to slip from the command of your imperial power, nor to wish to seek things that are not for the mind of your power. From this, it is necessary to kindle what was given into the hands of a pirate; what they did to him, the most holy men of Dyrrhachium destroyed. Behold the resulting decrees, especially those of self-intent. From this we know that it was necessary to be betrayed again. For from those things in the city, the same person seems to be a riddle to all, or what else is greater?

<term>Manuel</term>: Referring to Manuel I Komnenos (1143–1180), known for his legal reforms and military campaigns.
<term>Dyrrhachium</term>: A strategic coastal city, often the site of Norman invasions and pirate activity.
<term>Autocrat</term>: A title for the Byzantine Emperor, signifying absolute rule.
<term>Decrees</term>: Official imperial or ecclesiastical rulings (dogmata).
<term>Grace</term>: Divine favor (charis), a central concept in imperial legitimacy.
<term>Commander of the Army</term>: A translation of "Stratokrator," one of the Emperor's military titles.

<summary>This page contains a formal imperial address or legal ruling concerning the city of Dyrrhachium and the validity of oaths of allegiance, specifically touching upon succession and the role of the Emperor's son-in-law. It highlights the complex relationship between imperial power, church officials in the provinces, and the defense of the empire against threats like piracy.</summary>
<keywords>Manuel I Komnenos, Dyrrhachium, imperial succession, oaths of allegiance, Byzantine law, Archbishop of Dyrrhachium, pirates, political alliances</keywords>

# A fearsome day

<unclear>...</unclear> receive, and to give me in addition; Demetrios <gloss>101</gloss> inhabited <note>original: "oikēse," here used metaphorically to mean he possessed or took up residence in a state of mind</note> this confidence <gloss>104</gloss> <unclear>...</unclear> of the matter. The <term>Imperial Power</term> <note>original: "kratos," a standard term for the Emperor’s supreme authority or the state itself</note> <unclear>...</unclear> made a request regarding those who have died; let no one ever presume to have such power. But if he acts otherwise toward them, their souls shall be subject to the punishments <note>original: "kolaseōn," implying judicial or divine penalties</note> they owe.

**Demetrios, Dyrrhachium, Imperial Power, Souls, Punishments**

<summary>This page records a legal or administrative report concerning an individual named Demetrios and his dealings at the strategic port of Dyrrhachium, concluding with a stern warning about the limits of personal power and the inevitability of punishment.</summary>
<keywords>Demetrios, Dyrrhachium, Imperial Power, Byzantine law, soul, punishment, Manuel I Komnenos</keywords>
